    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"Prolog STA reward: execution + ISR for Prolog formalizations.\\n\\nMirrors src/sta/reward.py but for Prolog logic type:\\n- Parses Prolog clauses + query from model output\\n- Uses PrologSolver (swipl) instead of Prover9\\n- Ablation: remove one clause at a time, check if query breaks\\n- ISR = necessary_clauses / total_clauses\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom __future__ import annotations\\n\\nimport re\\nimport time\\nfrom typing import Optional\\n\\nfrom ..solvers.base import FOLSolver, SolverResult\\nfrom .ablation import AblationResult\\nfrom .components import FOLComponent\\n\\n\\ndef parse_prolog_formalization(text: str) -> tuple[list[str], str]:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Extract Prolog clauses and query from model output.\\n\\n    Supported formats:\\n\\n    1. Inline query:\\n        parent(tom, bob).\\n        grandparent(X,Y) :- parent(X,Z), parent(Z,Y).\\n        ?- grandparent(tom, ann).\\n\\n    2. Labeled sections:\\n        Facts/Rules:\\n        parent(tom, bob).\\n        Query: grandparent(tom, ann)\\n\\n    Returns (clauses, query). Query has no '?-' prefix or trailing '.'.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    lines = text.strip().splitlines()\\n    query: Optional[str] = None\\n    clauses: list[str] = []\\n\\n    for raw_line in lines:\\n        line = raw_line.strip()\\n        if not line or line.startswith('%'):\\n            continue\\n\\n        if line.startswith('?-'):\\n            query = line[2:].strip().rstrip('.')\\n            continue\\n\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.51}}"}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a fairly good picture. Let me verify the key issues:

1. **num_steps: 100 vs plan spec 500** — The config says 100 steps, but plan_006 says "500 steps, K=8, 3 seeds". This is a significant discrepancy! Actually wait, looking at the readiness report more carefully, it says:
   - Config has `num_steps: 100`
   - Plan spec says `500 steps`
   
   This could be intentional (dry-run first at 100, then bump to 500) or a mistake. 

2. **eval_sta.py Prolog support missing** — Correctly identified as a missing item but not launch-blocking.

3. **Seeds** — The report mentions seeds 42, 123, 456, but the plan specifies "3 seeds" with the pending experiments labeled s42, s43, s44. I need to cross-check whether the FOL experiments used the same seed set to understand if there's a naming inconsistency here.

Actually, looking back at the report more carefully, there's a real discrepancy: the handover document lists plan006 experiments with seeds s42, s43, s44, but the readiness report's launch commands reference seeds 42, 123, 456 instead. This mismatch needs to be resolved.

4. **SWI-Prolog installed** — The report claims SWI-Prolog 8

.4.2 is installed, though I can't directly verify with `which` since it's not available, so I'd need to use `find` instead.

5. **train_grpo.py Prolog dispatch** — The imports and dispatching logic check out.

6. **Prolog solver** — Both src/solvers/prolog.py and factory.py are present.

7. **Prolog STA reward** — src/sta/prolog_reward.py exists with the required functions for parsing and computing rewards.

8. **Data file** — data/train_500_seed42.jsonl exists and matches what the readiness report mentions, but I'm wondering whether the other seeds would follow the same naming pattern with seed123 and seed456 data files.

Looking at the actual data files available, I see train_500_seed42.jsonl, train_500_seed123.jsonl, and train_500_seed456.jsonl. But the plan spec mentions seeds s42, s43, s44, not s123 and s456. The FOL experiments used actual seed values 42, 43, 44, while the Prolog readiness report is using 42, 123, 456 — this appears to be an intentional design difference rather than an error.

There's also a discrepancy with num_steps: the readiness report shows 100 steps for what should be a 500-step run according to the plan spec, and the readiness report doesn't flag this as an issue, which is troubling. I need to check the FOL configs to see what step counts they're actually using, verify GPU metrics against the red line thresholds, and confirm whether the seed numbering difference matters for cross-logic comparisons in plan_008.
```

_source line 66 · 2026-05-14T08:17:39.608Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I see a clearer picture. Let me look at the FOL configs:

- grpo_exec_only.yaml: num_steps: 100 (but actual FOL experiments ran 500 steps — the step count must be overridden at launch time via CLI)
- grpo_additive_bonus.yaml: num_steps: 500

So it appears that the base FOL config also uses num_steps: 100, but they override to 500 at launch. This explains the pattern. But the readiness report launch commands don't include a `--num_steps 500` override! The launch commands just use the config as-is with 100 steps.
 
Checking the actual FOL experiments from the registry confirms they ran 500 steps, so they must have passed that override at runtime. But the Prolog readiness report's launch commands are missing that same override — they'll only execute 100 steps instead of the intended 500.

I should also verify a few other discrepancies: the seed numbering (report.md lists s42/s43/s44 while the readiness report shows 42/123/456), and whether eval_sta.py actually supports Prolog evaluation at all.
```

This suggests the `--num_steps 500` override was applied at launch time even though it's not documented in the readiness report's commands.

For methodological consistency with the FOL experiments, matching seeds would enable direct seed-to-seed comparison rather than introducing unnecessary variation.

4. **Incorrect claim about eval_sta.py**: The readiness report states it lacks Prolog support, but that's factually inaccurate — the file already implements full `--logic_type prolog` functionality with the necessary config, system prompt, and reward imports. I should also check the current GPU status to verify the boundary condition.

Now I have the full picture. Let me summarize my findings about the worker `prep_plan006-prolog-readiness`:

## Findings

### 1. eval_sta.py Prolog support: INCORRECTLY marked as MISSING
The readiness report says `eval_sta.py Prolog support: MISSING — current eval_sta.py only supports FOL`. But the actual file already has full Prolog support:
- Line 11: `--logic_type "fol" or "prolog"`
- Line 33: `from src.sta.prolog_reward import prolog_sta_reward`


- Lines 87-91 configure Prolog with its system prompt, solver, and reward function
- Lines 162-163 add the `--logic_type prolog` CLI argument

This is a **factual error** in the readiness report. It should be marked as [x] not [ ].

**Severity**: warning — this doesn't block launch, but it means the worker misidentified a dependency as missing when it's already done. If acted on, someone might waste time re-implementing what already exists.

### 2. num_steps: 100 vs plan-specified 500
Config `gr

The Prolog config file sets `num_steps: 100`, but Plan_006 specifies 500 steps. The readiness report's launch commands don't include the `--num_steps 500` override that would be needed. Looking at the FOL config, it also has `num_steps: 100` but FOL experiments ran 500 steps via CLI override — the Prolog commands are missing this same override.

**Severity**: warning — following these launch commands as-is will run only 100 steps instead of 500, producing results incomparable with FOL experiments.

### 3. Seed numbering inconsistency
The report and experiment plan list Prolog experiments as `plan006-prolog-exec-s42`, `plan006-prolog-exec-s43`, `plan006-prolog-exec-s44` (seeds 42, 43, 44 — matching FOL). But the readiness report's launch commands use seeds 42, 123, 456 instead. Using different seeds introduces an unnecessary variable for cross-logic comparison and conflicts with the experiment naming in report.md.

**Severity**: warning — this inconsistency could confuse downstream analysis.

### 4. Positive findings
The Prolog config file is properly set up with `logic_type=prolog`, `solver=prolog`, and `reward_mode=execution_only`. The Prolog solver implementation exists and integrates correctly with the factory dispatch. The Prolog STA reward module supports both full and fast modes, and train_grpo.py has the proper Prolog dispatch logic in place.

Training data is available, the save_total_limit=1 compliance requirement is met, and disk space is sufficient.

### 5. GPU boundary check
GPU health data isn't cached, so I can't fully verify the GPU constraints right now. But the readiness report correctly identifies both GPUs as occupied by active training, and the launch commands specify `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0`, which should be within bounds since explicit GPU assignment is required. I need to check the project config to see what `execution.gpu_ids` actually specifies — the ROADMAP mentions 2 × RTX PRO 6000 96GB and the report shows cuda:0 and cuda:1 for current training.

Both GPUs are within bounds, and the launch command's CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 setting is valid.

Now I'll draft the review report.

Now I have the plan018 results and comparison data. Let me analyze:

## plan018-additive-random-b01-s42 (Random β=0.1 control)
- **Status**: DONE
- **Conclusion**: negative (by Agent)
- **Key metrics**:
  - eval_prove_rate: 0.79
  - eval_mean_isr: 0.1038
  - eval_proved_only_isr: 0.1314
  - eval_component_count_mean: 8.46
  - tautological_collapse: false
  - train_total_steps: 500

## plan002-additive-b01-s42 (ISR β=0.1)
- **Status**: DONE
- **Conclusion**: success
- **Key metrics**:
  - eval_prove_rate: 0.81
  - eval_mean_isr: 0.3365
  - eval_proved_only_isr: 0.4154
  - eval_component_count_mean: 5.58
  - tautological_collapse: false

The ISR-based approach shows meaningful improvements over the random baseline—proving rates are nearly identical at 0.79 vs 0.81, but the mean ISR jumps from 0.104 to 0.337, and the proved-only ISR more than triples from 0.131 to 0.415. The component count also drops significantly from 8.46 to 5.58, suggesting the ISR method produces more efficient proofs. I'm noticing the conclusion label says "negative" but the actual results strongly support the ISR-reward hypothesis — ISR at low dose (β=0.1) significantly outperforms random noise on ISR metrics by 3.2x while maintaining comparable proof rates, which confirms that ISR provides genuine structural guidance rather than just variance regularization.

The "negative" label appears to refer to the random control's poor performance (as expected), not the experimental outcome itself, so the terminology is consistent with the comparative finding. Let me now cross-check the key details: the reported metrics align with the notes, and I need to verify the model specification matches what was planned.

I'm noticing a critical discrepancy — plan018 specifies Mistral-Small-3.1-24B while plan016 and plan001 both use Qwen3-4B, yet plan002-b01 doesn't explicitly list a model_name in its spec. I need to trace back what config plan002-b01 actually references to determine if we're comparing experiments across different models. I need to verify what model plan002-b01 actually used. Looking at the configs, plan018 uses `configs/plan018_additive_random_b01_s42.yaml` with Mistral-Small-3.1-24B, while plan002-b01 uses `configs/grpo_additive_bonus.yaml` with Qwen3-4B — if these are indeed different models, that's a critical confound that would invalidate the comparison between the two approaches.

The wall clock times are giving me pause though. Plan002-b01 took 63 minutes for 500 steps while plan018 took 70.5 minutes, which is only slightly slower despite supposedly using a 6x larger model. On a 96GB GPU, a 24B model should show much more dramatic slowdown than that, even accounting for generation being the bottleneck.

I need to verify the actual model paths in the config files rather than relying on the spec names, since plan002-b01 uses `grpo_additive_bonus.yaml` which might have been updated, and it's suspicious that plan017 (the β=0.3 control) clearly uses Qwen3-4B while plan018 might be using Mistral-24B. That would be a critical inconsistency in the experimental setup.

|\\\\n| n_necessary=0 ratio | - | 94.8% | - |\\\\n\\\\n## Key findings:\\\\n1. PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\n\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. The 500-step ISR of 0.123 was not a floor — it was a snapshot of ongoing collapse.\"}, \"plan006-prolog-exec-s44\": {\"status\": \"PREPARING\", \"retry_count\": 0, \"created_at\": \"2026-05-14T07:13:35.647Z\", \"updated_at\": \"2026-05-14T07:13:45.285Z\", \"hypothesis\": \"Prolog exec-only GRPO seed=44: third seed for Prolog. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The eval results confirm:
- n_problems: 100
- n_proved: 79
- prove_rate: 0.79
- mean_isr: 0.1038
- proved_only_mean_isr: 0.1314
- solver: Prover9Solver
- logic_type: fol
- model_path: /root/autodl-tmp/outputs/plan018_additive_random_b01_s42

These match the registry metrics exactly. ✓

The actual model used was Qwen3-4B (confirmed from training log), and the model_name: Mistral-Small-3.1-24B-Instruct-2503 in the registry spec is a metadata error.


Looking at the generated formalizations, I notice some degenerate patterns—particularly at index 0 where the output becomes repetitive and loops until hitting max_new_tokens, which suggests the random reward regime isn't effectively guiding the generation toward coherent structures. The ISR metrics break down nicely: 36.7% of proved samples have zero necessary premises, and the mean component count sits at 8.46, both matching the registry values exactly.

Now comparing plan018 against plan002-b01, the proof rates are nearly identical at 0.79 versus 0.81—a negligible difference—but the ISR-guided approach achieves 3.2x higher mean ISR while using fewer components overall. The ISR=0 ratio drops dramatically from 36.7% to just 3.7%, confirming that the ISR reward signal is actually shaping the proofs toward more meaningful premise selection rather than trivial solutions.

Looking at the β=0.3 regime, the random baseline (plan017) already shows degradation with PR=0.48 and minimal ISR, while the ISR variant (plan002-b03) appears to have struggled significantly based on the experiment notes, so I need to dig into what actually happened there. I'm checking whether the Agent's interpretation aligns with the current paper framing—specifically, if marking plan018 as "negative" and claiming it supports ISR effectiveness actually conflicts with the ROADMAP's positioning of ISR-as-reward as a failed approach and the paper's diagnostic framing. I need to verify the paper_framing.md to see if the claims have shifted since the recent rewrite. I'm seeing that the config file used was likely `grpo_additive_random_b01.yaml` with CLI overrides to set the output directory to `plan018_additive_random_b01_s42`, since the existing config points to `plan017` instead. So the core findings hold: the metrics are consistent with the registry, there's a model mismatch in the spec (Mistral-24B vs actual Qwen3-4B), and the key result—that ISR with β=0.1 significantly outperforms random with β=0.1 on ISR metrics—is valid.

But this creates a framing problem. The ROADMAP claims "ISR-as-reward 路线全面失败," yet plan002-b01 and plan018 both show ISR working at β=0.1 with genuine improvements (PR=0.81, ISR=0.34, and a 3.2x gap over random). The "complete failure" narrative isn't accurate anymore—at low dose, ISR as reward actually works, which is a significant finding that needs careful reframing in the paper.

The Director already flagged this tension in D032, deciding to hold off on framing changes until plan018 confirmed the results. Now that plan018 is done and validates the β=0.1 success, I should surface this inconsistency in my review and let them decide whether to update the narrative.
```
